- f1: Attacked by Rook d1. (King cannot move here)
Wait, is there any other square?
- e1: (The current square).
The only way to get out of check is to capture the checker or move to a safe square.
- Capturing the rook at d1:
  - e1xd1: Illegal, d1 is attacked by Bishop c2.
  - c1xd1: Illegal, Bishop at c1 cannot move to d1.
  - e2xd1: Illegal, Knight at e2 cannot move to d1.

  - f1: Attacked by Rook at d1.
  - f2: Attacked by Pawn at e3.
  - d2: Attacked by Rook at d1.
  - d1: Attacked by Rook at d1 and Bishop at c2.

Wait, this is checkmate!
Let me just re-verify the Knight's moves one more time.
Knight e2:
(5,2) -> (4,4), (6,4), (3,3), (7,3), (3,1), (7,1), (4,0), (6,0).
d1 is (4,1).
Is (4,1) in the list? No.
Wait, (4,1) is d1. Let me check again.
(e,2) is (5,2).
Knight move is (±1, ±2) or (±2, ±1).
(5-1, 2-2) = (4,0)
(5-1, 2+2) = (4,4)
(5+1, 2-2) = (6,0)
(5+1, 2+2) = (6,4)
(5-2, 2-1) = (3,1) -> c1
(5-2, 2+1) = (3,3) -> c3
(5+2, 2-1) = (7,1) -> g1
(5+2, 2+1) = (7,3) -> g3
So the Knight at e2 can move to c1, c3, g1, g3.
It cannot move to d1.

Let me re-verify the Bishop at c1.
Bishop c1 is (3,1).
d1 is (4,1).
This is a horizontal move. Bishop cannot move horizontally.

Let me re-verify the King at e1 capture of Rook at d1.
King e1 is (5,1).
Rook d1 is (4,1).
They are adjacent. The King can capture.
HOWEVER, the square d1 is also attacked by the Bishop at c2.
Bishop c2 is (3,2).
d1 is (4,1).
(4-3, 1-2) = (1, -1).
This is a diagonal move!
Yes, the Bishop at c2 attacks d1.
So the King cannot capture the Rook at d1 because it would be moving into check from the Bishop at c2.

This is checkmate!
